ParlaMint-FI_2017-12-19-ps-145.u99	Presentation of Section 32 on the administrative sector of the Ministry of Employment and the Economy. — — — — — — — — — — — — — — — — — — — — Representative, Section President Juhantalo.
ParlaMint-FI_2017-12-19-ps-145.u100	Mr President! The Section for Employment and the Economy has dealt with 13 different themes, and in this context I would like to express my warm thanks to the members of the section — both to the government parties and, in particular, to the opposition parties. We have had a very good spirit and a hard working atmosphere, and we have had a good assistant, and we have also received assistance from the entire staff of the Committee on Finance in our work. In our view, the appropriations proposed by the section do not seem to be very strong enough to hit the main category of the Ministry of Employment and the Economy. That is why we also need such an administrative agreement with a few other sections, because we are right on the line here where this additional money, which we will then be distributing at the turn of the year, is particularly targeted. We have heard the responsible ministers — both Minister Lindström and Lintilaa — and some 50 experts, and we have received a number of written statements. During this year we have paid particular attention to SMEs, export growth and internationalisation, including Russia, which has always played an important role, and the risks of future reforms. We have raised the issue of growth service reform, the creation of Business Finland, which is very diverse and hopefully gives a lot of new insights to that sector: internationalisation and opportunities for development of exports, research, development and innovation, positive developments in the employment situation and the development of integration. And there is still a great deal to be done in the employment of immigrants, among other things. We have now drawn attention to two different issues, the first of which is the importance of tourism. It has gradually become more and more widely considered in Finland, meaning that it really matters. It can play an even greater role in economic growth and employment. There are about 29,000 companies in the tourism sector and they employ 140,000 people. That is a very large number, which is rarely seriously thought of. At the moment, our tourism income is EUR 14 billion, and it is quite easy to increase it to EUR 20 billion — a very large sum. As we all know now, the interest in Finland as a tourist country has increased and should be exploited through fast, well-designed and customer-oriented measures. Now it is positive, as we have said, that growth is faster than in other Nordic countries, and the only problem is that we have fallen so far behind in the past. In other Nordic countries, not only the strength of the national economy, but also the ability of companies to invest in larger investments and efforts is clearly still there and better than ours. The second is the problem of matching labour supply and demand. Our problem has only worsened, and one third of the establishments have recruitment problems, making it difficult for the technology industry, the construction sector and the tourism industry. There are also development challenges and the need to improve the efficiency of the process in obtaining skilled labour from abroad. It is generally acknowledged and stated that the government has taken action on a number of issues raised by my section in recent years as new problems. That is very positive, but it must then be said that the reform of business subsidies has not received enough resources and has not made any progress. As regards 32, TeJ has proposed the following additional appropriations: to promote tourism in the archipelago of 400000 coastal regions — this is the whole region from Tornio to the river Estonia — EUR 200000 to Viexpo for business export projects, EUR 150000 for the exploitation of by-products from the food industry, EUR 150000 for the purpose of improving the reuse of scrap waste from the insulating plastic industry, EUR 150000 for the purpose of finding good procedures for the processing of nutrients into higher processing rates, fertilisers containing recycled nutrients, EUR 100000 for the establishment of a restructuring unit for the TE Offices and EUR 50000 for support for the activities of the Consumer Union. And, as I said at the outset, we have gone through a number of important appropriations so that they have not hit the framework of the Ministry of the Environment and the Committee on Agriculture and Forestry, and we thank the Committee on the Environment, Public Health and Consumer Protection for having received such an understanding and for having supported their project. We have handed it over to them, but as a project suitable for our sector. — Thank you, Mr President.
ParlaMint-FI_2017-12-19-ps-145.u101	Mr President! There has been a long-awaited turn in our economy. The outlook is positive throughout Finland and the growth is wide-ranging. My estimate is that our economic growth will continue to be strong next year. The Ministry of Finance's estimate published today also points to this, which predicts an increase of 3.1% for this year and an increase of 2.4% for next year. Positive economic development has been reflected in a strong increase in productivity and a pick-up in exports. The background is the acceleration of investment after ten years of investment recession. The competitiveness pact and the reduction in unit labour costs through it have played a crucial role in this. This is a great thank you to the social partners, who were able to make difficult decisions. The government's economic policy has three main priorities: first, improving employment, second, modernising businesses and supporting growth, and thirdly, getting public finances on a sound footing. Without higher employment, we will not succeed in getting public finances on a solid footing. The improvement in employment, in turn, requires that our industry is able to renew itself and thus to employ more people. The government will continue to pursue an employment policy that has led to more and more unemployed people getting jobs. Employment trends are in the right direction and the pace of travel has improved. The problem of matching job vacancies and unemployed job seekers is real and calls for new measures, just as the section president said in his recent speech. There is no such thing as a silver bullet, but we need solutions that are widely related to the movement of labour, skills, ability to work and incentive traps. Although many in the Chamber sometimes seem to wish the opposite, government gloves do not fall and hands do not freeze. Work will also be done on the achievement of the employment objective next spring. It is therefore particularly important to support the growth and renewal of businesses in order to strengthen their ability to employ. New entrepreneurs and skilled employees enable Finland to succeed. We must ensure that the financing of growth works and that the business environment is competitive. The draft budget for 2018 for the Ministry of Employment and the Economy responds well to the needs of business growth and internationalisation. We have prepared a Business Finland reform to bring together the services of Tekes and Finpro. Business Finland lowers the threshold for taking up innovation, product development and internationalisation services when they will be found under the same roof in the future. We are putting forward additional efforts to support growth, internationalisation and innovation policy. Business Finland's grant and loan mandates will increase by a total of EUR 40 million. In addition, a completely new EUR 30 million capital loan will be made available to Business Finland to finance start-up, industrial renewal and development companies. The development of tourism is one of the government's growth leaders. The Tourism 4.0 project will be supported by EUR 8 million next year, of which just under EUR 5 million will be included in the operating expenses of Business Finland, the Ministry of Employment and the Economy. Mr President! Finally, I would like to thank the Committee on Finance and its Section for Employment and the Economy for their good and constructive preparation of the budget. The additional appropriations allocated to the Ministry of Employment and the Economy contribute well to the objectives of growth, exports and renewal.
ParlaMint-FI_2017-12-19-ps-145.u102	Secretary Lindström, five minutes.
ParlaMint-FI_2017-12-19-ps-145.u103	Mr President! As Minister of Economic Affairs Lintilä pointed out above, there has been a long-awaited turn in our economy. Both this year and the coming years will look more positive than we have been accustomed to in the last decade. Positive economic growth provides a good basis for promoting our employment situation. So far, employment developments have not been as strong as our economic growth suggests, and this signals the mobilisation of under-utilised resources. We have been determined in the government to ensure that our employment situation improves. Through our actions, we need to strengthen the employment opportunities of our companies and then ensure skilled labour and access to them. The Ministry of Finance has estimated that the unemployment rate would fall to 8.1 per cent next year and to 7.8 in 2019. It's the right direction. In this process of development, the government's efforts to improve employment play an important role. More and more unemployed people have been employed. Of course, there is still a long way to go to the employment target set by the government. In the latest forecast of the Ministry of Employment and the Economy, the growth in employment in the current year is estimated at 15,000 persons. With good economic development and measures to support employment development, the pace will accelerate next year, when employment growth is estimated to be 25,000. This trend would lead to an employment rate of more than 70%. According to the latest employment survey, employment and economic development offices had 272,500 unemployed job seekers at the end of October, which is over 56,000 fewer than one year ago. The sharp fall in unemployment in unemployment statistics has been due both to the cyclical shift and to the updating of the statistics by the periodic interviews of the unemployed and, in some respects, to this one-off pension solution, which also bears the name of the signatory. Compared with one year ago, there were more than 13,000 more vacancies at the end of October than one year earlier. We have increased the government's efforts. At the end of October, the activation rate was 31.6 per cent, which is 3.5 percentage points more than one year ago. By increasing the number of fixed-term interviews, we have already reached the point where two thirds of the unemployed have up-to-date employment plans. The change from the beginning of the year is significant. At that time, 18 per cent of the unemployed had been interviewed on a fixed-term basis before the duration of three months of unemployment. The purpose of the interviews is to serve and support the unemployed in seeking employment better than before. Well, new measures to improve employment have been prepared and will enter into force. Let me mention a few: we improve job seekers' ability to develop their skills without losing unemployment benefit, we facilitate the right of the unemployed to start business with unemployment benefit, we establish the Ohjaamo business and we expand the Ohjaamo service package, we increase performance-based purchases with the aim of directing the 10,000 under 30-year-olds to new services, we increase the provision of pay support in interviews with the under 30-year-olds, and we combine the provision of pay support with job search and job coaching. Mr President! I, too, would like to thank the Committee on Finance and its Section for Employment and the Economy for the work you have done and, in particular, for the debate that took place in the section. I thought that was very constructive and appropriate. — — — — Thank you.
ParlaMint-FI_2017-12-19-ps-145.u104	It is now an opportunity to ask for a 1-minute reply, and they will begin by Mrs Filatov.
ParlaMint-FI_2017-12-19-ps-145.u105	Mr President! We are all delighted that there is some light in the economy and in employment after a long difficult situation. But when the so-called active model of unemployment protection was adopted here today, which actually cuts unemployment security and gives the state the right to be passive in the provision of active measures or at least does not require it to be offered, I would ask the Minister: how do you intend to cope with the economic equation that it has become clear in the committees that the resources we have earmarked for active measures are not enough to secure the measures needed to implement this model? The second question actually relates to these interviews. They are a good thing, they have been done in time, then, because of the lack of resources, they have not been done, and now, as it were, an organisation has been caught up to make them. They must be designed to help the unemployed, but now, unfortunately, many unemployed people do not even notice that he has been interviewed because it has been done electronically.
ParlaMint-FI_2017-12-19-ps-145.u106	Mr President! As Minister Lintilä said, we have good economic growth at the moment and exports are pulling. But we are coming to a situation where we are beginning to have the capacity of industry almost fully operational, and in order to be able to respond to this volume of exports, we should also be able to invest in new production capacity. Then, on the other hand, we are dealing with business subsidies and thinking about cleaning them up. I would like to ask the Minister when you did not mention these business subsidies in the opening speech, what kind of view does the Minister have of the fact that, when business subsidies are rightly considered, it would be possible to maintain international competitiveness and, at the same time, promote the investments that we need. ParlaMint-FI_2017-12-19-ps-145.u218	Mr President! Thank you to the section president and to the entire Committee on Finance. Indeed, the administrative sector of the Ministry of Social Affairs and Health in the state budget proposal for 2018 amounts to EUR 15 billion, which is just over a quarter of the state budget. This appropriation is approximately EUR 400 million higher than the actual budget for 2017. The increase in expenditure is due, inter alia, to the exercise of freedom of choice and to changes in the needs assessment and the impact of previous decisions such as the Competitiveness Pact for 2018. The draft budget focuses on the livelihoods of the most vulnerable. This can be seen, among other things, in the form of increases in minimum sickness allowance and guarantee pension. In order to safeguard the primacy of basic security in relation to income support, the minimum amount of sickness allowance, parental allowance and rehabilitation allowance will be increased. This is to ensure that the beneficiary does not have to rely on basic income support. The increase will increase central government expenditure by approximately EUR 8 million. Mr President! Pension provision will be improved by allocating EUR 18 million to the increase in the guaranteed pension and related benefits. The guarantee pension is estimated to increase by EUR 15 per month. The rehabilitation allowance for young people and the minimum amount of rehabilitation paid during vocational rehabilitation will also be increased in order to promote the employment of those who are able to work part-time. The basic allowance for care allowance for pensioners will be increased to EUR 71 per month, which will increase central government expenditure by EUR 10 million. Travel expenses and travel brokerage activities are renewed due to the liberalisation of taxi services and an additional EUR 2 million has been earmarked. In health insurance, the biggest change in euro is due to the effects of the competitiveness agreement. Under the agreement, the reduction in employers' social security contributions and the reduction in the contribution burden for the lowest-income earners will increase the need for funding by EUR 136 million. The operating costs of the Social Insurance Institution will be increased by EUR 5 million, which will be allocated, inter alia, to the treatment of the benefit of basic income and the implementation of the active model of unemployment security. An appropriation of 2.7 billion is proposed for unemployment benefit, which is EUR 153 million less than in the 2017 actual budget. This is mainly due to the projected fall in the unemployment rate. The active model to be introduced to increase employment will reduce the need for appropriations in Section STM by EUR 9.5 million. The active model also encourages the unemployed to accept short-term work or to participate in employment-promoting services for a short period of time. The number of days at the start of unemployment is reduced from 7 to 5 days. The reference period for unemployment is three months, and each time you look at it, there will be one monthly take-over day, which can be avoided by being active. The activity requirement is met if you are employed for one week or a total of 18 hours during the reference period or you are in the employment-promoting service for five days. Business start-ups during unemployment will be facilitated in order to promote employment. In addition, short-term studies will be made possible without losing unemployment benefit, which will promote vocational capacity-building and business start-ups. These changes will increase the appropriation requirement by EUR 13.5 million. An appropriation of EUR 4 million has been earmarked for the development of vocational rehabilitation and the renewal of the criteria. In addition, the appropriation for the wage subsidy will be increased by EUR 14.1 million. Mr President! An amount of EUR 183 million is proposed for the operating expenditure of the administrative offices and institutions of the Ministry of Social Affairs and Health, which takes into account savings measures totalling EUR 3 million. The institutional structure of the administrative sector has been developed by combining the Social Security Appeal Board and the Employment Protection Appeal Board into the Social Security Appeal Board. EUR 358 million will be allocated to grants distributed through STEA to promote health and social well-being. This is an increase of EUR 40 million compared to the current budget. EUR 190 million will be allocated to support veterans, of which EUR 41.7 million will be allocated to the rehabilitation of front veterans. The appropriations for rehabilitation will remain at the level of the actual budget for 2017. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-FI_2017-12-19-ps-145.u378	Madam President! When Mr Savio addressed the question of the cost of immigration to me, a few words should be mentioned. This immigration is something which, in economic terms, is very often explicitly a minus sign — how much less, then it is a little more difficult to calculate, because there are, of course, costs that are easy to calculate, which are immediate costs, such as reception costs and the various costs involved, but, of course, the more relevant ones are the cumulative costs that are more difficult to calculate. If a number of people come into European societies who are very difficult to integrate into our labour market, which, however, require a high level of education and language skills, and if we quickly come from a very distant culture and our people are not integrated into our labour market, then the cumulative costs may, of course, be very high. In addition, of course, there will be a wide range of social effects for which there may not be a price in euro at all. That is why I say that it is actually more relevant than making such calculations is specifically to pursue a sensible and sustainable immigration policy that avoids the creation of such costs.

ParlaMint-FI_2017-12-19-ps-145.u387	Mr President! An appropriation of EUR 2.872 billion is proposed for the administrative sector of the Ministry of Defence. This is approximately EUR 42.3 million more than in the 2017 budget. The share of expenditure in the section is estimated to be around 1.24% of GDP. The Committee is pleased that the draft budget for the administrative sector takes full account of the needs outlined in the defence report. On the basis of the reports received by the committee, the objectives set for the administrative sector can be achieved by means of the proposed budget. Operating expenditure of the Defence Forces. About EUR 2.426 billion are proposed for military defence. Of this EUR 1.941 billion will be allocated to the Defence Forces' operational expenditure. The proposal includes an increase of EUR 50 million to improve the preparedness required for changes in the security environment. It is also positive that operating expenditure includes an additional EUR 2 billion for the recruitment of contract soldiers. The appropriation for the draft budget will enable 18000 re-engagement exercises to be carried out, as the objective has been set. The committee considers it justified to increase the total funding for research and development in line with the objective of achieving a 2% level of military defence expenditure in 2021. Defence equipment procurements. An appropriation of approximately EUR 478 million is proposed for this article. The acquisition of defence equipment also includes an appropriation of EUR 4.6 million for the Hawk Mk 51 modifier. The actual acquisition of military equipment accounts for about 20 per cent of the military defence budget, excluding VAT. This is still lower than before the military reform. Mr President! The committee welcomes the fact that the proposal includes an increase in the budget set out in the Government Programme for the correction of performance deficiencies. This appropriation will increase from EUR 80 million in the current year to EUR 110 million in 2018. The order mandate of the article includes almost all of the Navy's strategic performance project Fleet 2020. This is worth EUR 1.01 billion. It is necessary for Finland to be able to ensure sufficient expertise at home to enable the system acquired to be used, maintained and, if necessary, modified throughout its life cycle in all circumstances. The committee also draws attention to the cost-savings sought by joint Nordic procurements and welcomes the fact that the first steps towards concrete cooperation have been taken. Finally, Mr President, support for the activities of defence organisations. The committee very much welcomes the fact that the government has finally taken account of Parliament's will and has not cut the budget for this item. This has been a tradition in this Parliament for too long. Now it's fixed, and this line is good. Finally, Mr President, I would like to thank Minister Niinistö for having done your job well. We have had a very good consensus in the section on the activities of the Minister and we welcome all the work you have done so far. At this stage, we should also thank the section for the fact that it has been very unanimous in its work on the budget in this section. — — — — Thank you.

This is, of course, a positive development, and even if it does not solve our acute problems, Finland should of course be involved in this, so that, in the event of a tight spot, there are means to apply.

ParlaMint-FI_2017-12-19-ps-145.u467	Madam President! As has already been said, it is good to note that equipment is never an absolute value. Defence expenditure increases are a pan-European trend to respond to the deteriorating security situation. Finland cannot be left behind in this development. After major strategic projects over the coming years, Finland’s defence budget will increase considerably. Even now, the budget is quite comparable internationally, but the method of calculation differs. If it were to be calculated by the method of calculation used by NATO, defence expenditure would also include the maintenance costs of the crisis management forces, which are now in the section of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, the pensions paid to staff, thereby excluding the employer's pension contributions, and the costs of the Border Guard. With this NATO method of calculating the GDP share of Finnish defence expenditure will be approximately 1.6 per cent in 2017. When implemented, strategic performance projects will raise Finland’s defence budget to around 2% by international criteria calculated in 2021. The Assembly's parliamentary group proposed a change in the way in which the defence statement was discussed, and the Committee on Defence also recorded this in its report. Of course, Mr Orpo, the Minister for Finance, deserves special thanks for the fact that there will no longer be a cut in support of defence organisations this year. In previous years, the cut amount has been returned to Parliament's reading, and Parliament has always required that the matter be taken into account in the next budget. Voluntary defence activities are extremely important in maintaining the general will and skills of the country. This is a true Finland 100, and for that I would like to thank the Minister for Finance very much.

It is good that next year a separate budget has been set aside to strengthen environmental health research. The changes have also reduced the comfort of cottages. The Ministry of the Environment has published an updated monitoring guide for peat production in October of this year. Finally, I would have asked the Minister: what is the current situation with regard to monitoring environmental permits for peat production and how to ensure that a revised monitoring guide has been introduced in the activities of the authorities?

Emissions trading compensation is targeted at sectors that already receive a high level of support through reduced electricity tax rates and tax refunds for energy intensive industries. According to the Emissions Trading Directive and the Council of Europe's approach, at least half of the revenues from that auction should be allocated to climate action. This was done in the past and, among other things, funded climate action in developing countries. In addition to Canada and the UK, for example, Finland has been creating such an international coalition out of coal, which is moving towards carbon-free energy production. It seems that this is really necessary, because otherwise the use of coal is only growing. The second thing that caused that increase in emissions in 2016 was that the share of the renewable component in transport fuels decreased. After all, we have a gradual upward blending obligation, but producers have used that renewable component more and more, depending on the market situation, so that is beyond the objective set by that law. Then 2016 came down again, when traffic emissions increased due to this reduction in the blending rate. This highlights what Mr Vehkaperä pointed out, that when transport emissions need to be driven down quickly, all the means are needed. We want to kick off electronic traffic by developing charging networks as well as purchasing electric cars. We want to promote the use of gas in transport and, in fact, these biogas projects have now been heavily supported in recent years, although many of them are still under construction, and there will certainly be more support next year. But those rapid emission reductions occur in such a way that the existing fleet of cars can get fuel with lower emissions into its tanks, i.e. by mixing the renewable component. Last night, in the Environment Council, we reached agreement on the Renewable Energy Directive. That harmony in the case of renewable liquid fuels in transport is good. It guarantees the continuity of the raw material base that can be used for the production of renewable liquid fuels at present and ensures that at EU level it will also become a binding target for these advanced fuels, which are specifically used and produced in Finland. This now gives us the necessary investment certainty, and in Finland too we are moving forward quickly to get that mixed-up legislation next year. There is a working group phase in progress, a legislative process is about to begin, and now these decisions at EU level provide a good basis for it. Decarbonisation, zero-emission transport — that is what this government is doing, and biogas is being promoted. We are committed to implementing the objectives of the Paris climate agreement and carbon neutrality in 2045.

Justice and moderation are considered the need for mandatory changes in the perspective of actual victims, i.e. riverside people and migratory fish. ParlaMint-FI_2017-12-19-ps-145.u541	Mr President! Members of the Committee on the Environment, Mr Kosonen and Mr Multala, and also Mr Kopra, have already made a good comment on Mr Mäkelä, but I would like to add something to this. Now, the most visible and ugly plastic garbage in the human eye is the big plastic garbage the eye sees, but the most environmentally dangerous is microplastic, and even the big plastic garbage in the ocean, for example, will then grind into this microplastic over time. And really, Finland is not free from this problem: for example, when the Baltic Sea beaches have been studied, the Finnish shores have not stood out in any way, especially as cleaner than neighbouring countries from plastic waste, rather the opposite. But what many do not know — I learned it myself in the European Parliament, because it dealt with EU legislation aimed at reducing the use of plastic bags — is that microplastics in the sea, and probably also in lakes, absorb organic environmental poisons, the concentration of which on the surface of plastic waste may be up to a million times higher than the concentration of these same toxins in the water itself. This means that, for example, when we eat crustaceans that have eaten microplastics, or when we eat fish that has eaten microplastics, they also contain these toxic substances for humans, and of course this is an extremely dangerous matter for marine ecosystems. I am pleased that several colleagues have stressed the seriousness of this issue. Finally, I would like to say that I agree with Mr Mäkelä on one point, namely that in construction, weather protection should be standard practice.
